[5/26/2024 10:06 AM] tilinets
Xylem in a petal of an Iris flower. Xylem is one of the two types of transport tissue in vascular plants, the other being phloem. The basic function of the xylem is to transport water from roots to stems and leaves, but it also transports nutrients. The word xylem is derived from the Ancient Greek word ξύλον (xylon), meaning "wood"; the best-known xylem tissue is wood, though it is found throughout a plant.

[5/30/2024 10:43 AM] tilinets
**DID YOU KNOW?** 
Plants belonging to the Asteraceae family, like the *Bellis perennis* in the pictures below, don’t have a single flower but an inflorescence. The parts usually addressed as petals (the white ones) are in fact flowers themselves, just like the little yellow ones in the center.

[6/11/2024 3:28 PM] phototroph
Changing one gene may not have the expected result.  Proteins made by genes are often part of more extensive metabolic pathways that do the thing you are trying to mod. 
...Life extension? There are several billion dollar companies trying to do this right now for mice, humans. https://www.calicolabs.com/
If you **are** successful you could make a fortune.

[6/11/2024 5:21 PM] phototroph
Gamma rays and other radiation are dangerous. You don't want to try this without really extensive study. 
You at least need to know about radiation. Take a radiology class.  Then don't do it in your "HOME" ... you will need a dedicated lab environment. 
That stuff is really dangerous. The levels of radiation are higher than just UV rays(which are dangerous too). 
This is not something to take lightly. It can randomly alter YOUR genes too...cancer, anyone?


[6/11/2024 5:23 PM] phototroph
It also will make RANDOM changes... The gene pool is so big that most of the time you wouldn't be able to see any difference as most of the genes are silent.

[6/11/2024 6:36 PM] duffmint
I think there are a few big challenges with using CRISPR: 
1. Off-targeting makes it hard to ensure you're only editing one gene 
2. Selection and regeneration of plants from a single cell 
3. Must integrate CRISPR and a recombinase cassette into the cell along with a PCR amplicon or gene block (unless you're doing a gene knockout)
There's also the consideration that plant transfection techniques are not universal across all species. I'd probably lean towards electroporation because it can be more universally applied and will cause only transient expression of CRISPR and the recombinase but stable expression of the desired transgene. 
CRISPR cassettes are regulated and difficult to purchase. You can make them yourself but it's not fun. Institutions can buy them for $100.


[6/11/2024 6:39 PM] buzzin420
i have no direct knowledge of plant mutations but me and few others are experimenting with UV exposure to isolated monokaryon mushroom colony's and then breading with that gene pool once that combination undergoes mitosis (GEN1) we then grow out the spores and selectively breed mutated traits. obviously mushrooms have a very quick turn around in terms of breeding and your more so just hoping to win the lottery and solidify that trait with selective breeding. as far as extending plant life are you saying you just want to change the plant back to a photo period? your best tactic IMO for a home grower would be finding a photo period plant that is very similar to your auto and selectively breeding and back crossing to achieve a some what identical photo period without the need for gene splicing etc.. just use nature and a good eye. another fool proof way to keep the autoflower genetic line without losing it to senesces is just spraying lower branches with STS and self pollinating for fem seeds or keeping that pollen for your backcrossing

[6/14/2024 12:16 AM] phototroph
I had this DNA Extraction for plant tissues document made for general use. This can prepare a person to do PCR on a plant for 100% positive ID of a species. The result of a PCR test can be sent to a lab to get a string of DNA sequence that can be searched on a public database of known sequences.

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1250936757931999343/DNA_Extraction_Guide.docx?ex=6af2eee4&is=6af19d64&hm=6abfdb1a4fc454cb9fb8d6445c378e638032cd8014a723946c7c631fe190048a&


[6/14/2024 12:17 AM] phototroph
The only catch is that you have to have a thermo-cycler. These can be found for around $200. The chemicals to do the PCR are around $50-100.

[7/2/2024 3:06 PM] mralexzii
Hey all 🤠 , in my last semester in my biotechnology degree i picked to do a project related to explant and seed sterilization before they go into agar or liquid media.
I'm reaching out to to gather insights on the sterilization process. Your expertise and experience are invaluable, and I'd greatly appreciate your input on a few key questions. 
1. What are the main challenges you face in the sterilization of plant material?
•    Are there specific contaminants that are particularly difficult to eliminate?
•    Do you encounter any issues with consistency or reliability in your current methods?
2. How much time and resources are currently dedicated to the sterilization process?
•    On average, how long does it take to sterilize your plant material?
•    What kind of resources (equipment, personnel, etc.) are primarily involved in this process?
3. What features would you find most valuable in an automated sterilization system?
•    Are there any specific functionalities or capabilities that would significantly improve your workflow?
•    How important are factors like ease of use, cost-effectiveness, or scalability?
Your feedback will be extremely helpful for understanding the current landscape and identifying areas for improvement. I'm eager to hear about your experiences and your feedback will help me engineer my project
Thank you in advance for your time and insights! 

ive been doing plant tissue culture for the last couple of month as a hobby and wanted to do it more profetionaly with this project so Thank you again for your time and insights


[7/2/2024 5:38 PM] seedwasher
What are the main challenges you face in the sterilization of plant material?
•    Are there specific contaminants that are particularly difficult to eliminate?
Any type of fungus associated with seeds.

•    Do you encounter any issues with consistency or reliability in your current methods?
Not really, it works without issue 99% of the time.

How much time and resources are currently dedicated to the sterilization process?
10 minutes (or more) agitation with bleach solution + some Triton-X, 15-20 minutes rinsing with sterile water.

•    On average, how long does it take to sterilize your plant material?
30 minutes.

•    What kind of resources (equipment, personnel, etc.) are primarily involved in this process?
Benchtop vortexer and micro pipette. It is a semi-automated process.

What features would you find most valuable in an automated sterilization system?
•    Are there any specific functionalities or capabilities that would significantly improve your workflow?
Being able to adjust for specific sterilization duration, strength (in terms of rpm/speed).

•    How important are factors like ease of use, cost-effectiveness, or scalability?
Ease of use: Sterilization should be a simple process. I wouldn't like having to press too many buttons just to wash some seeds.
Cost-effectiveness: This process shouldn't cost more than the electricity and required waiting time.ap on number.
Scalability:  Not as crucial if the sterilization process is quick. I can simply run multiple separate batches through the automated sterilization system if there is a limit.

[7/31/2024 3:20 AM] asanford1984
"The exciting breakthrough came after experimentation with mebendazole, or MBZ, a medication commonly used to treat parasites, as detailed by Anthropocene.

Scientists found that plants treated with MBZ developed shallower roots than desired, and that helped them narrow in on which enzymes to suppress to grow plants with deeper, less horizontal roots."
https://www.thecooldown.com/green-tech/mebendazole-plants-deeper-roots-medicine-research/?fbclid=IwY2xjawEWcHhleHRuA2FlbQIxMQABHd5iAvogB8GS3r7C8cjv5vVYAw9Ms8Na2TSdGLiA0Ctd8uU6oZG1REm6gA_aem_j3fBniNqUcjjMY3MMp_uRQ

[9/11/2024 2:21 AM] asanford1984
Yes, it's true that palm trees are not considered "true trees" in the strict botanical sense. While palms are commonly referred to as trees because of their size and structure, they are technically monocots, which means they belong to the same category as grasses, lilies, and bamboo.

Here are a few key points:

1) Family : Palms belong to the 'Arecaceae' family, which is distinct from the families of most true trees.

2) Monocots : Palms are 'monocots', meaning they have one seed leaf, unlike most trees, which are dicots (having two seed leaves).

3) Growth : Palms lack the 'secondary growth' (woody tissue) typical of most trees. Instead of growing outward by adding layers of wood, palms grow vertically from a single point at the top (a meristem), and their trunk does not expand over time as woody trees do.

While palms share many external characteristics with trees, such as having a tall trunk and a canopy of leaves, their internal structure and growth patterns are more aligned with grasses.

#tree #usa #nature
https://www.allaboutpalmtrees.com/post/are-palm-trees-grass-or-trees

https://www.allaboutpalmtrees.com/post/are-palm-trees-grass-or-trees

[9/15/2024 9:20 PM] erwin_myco
I've made a lot of bioluminescent jars of Panellus stipticus on fine wood chips. Fun to look at, and glowed for at least a year. But it's a very low amount of light, I needed to acclimate my eyes for a little while to see it. After that it's nice to look at. A friend seemed to be able to see it immediatly when walking into a dark bathroom. So it differs per person, but I don't think it's a very usable amount of light for most practical uses. Although I have thought about using some type of mirror/lens to focus it towards a single point.

[9/15/2024 11:49 PM] asanford1984
Tips for Grafting Fruit Trees correctly
Materials Needed
Sharp grafting knife
Grafting tape or rubber bands
Rootstock and scion wood
Pruning shears
Rubbing alcohol (for sterilization)
Grafting sealant or wax
Step-by-Step Guide
1. Choosing Rootstock and Scion
Rootstock: Select healthy, disease-resistant rootstock compatible with the scion variety.
Scion: Choose scion wood from a healthy, productive tree, ensuring it matches the rootstock diameter and has several buds.
2. Timing
Best Season: Late winter or early spring before buds swell.
3. Preparing the Tools
Sterilize Tools: Clean with rubbing alcohol to prevent disease transmission.
4. Making the Cuts
Rootstock Cut: Make a clean cut on the rootstock (whip and tongue, cleft, or bark graft depending on size).
Scion Cut: Match the cut on the scion to the rootstock. For whip and tongue grafts, make a diagonal cut with a small slit.
5. Joining Rootstock and Scion
Align Cambium Layers: Carefully align the green cambium layers.
Secure the Graft: Bind with grafting tape or rubber bands to ensure good contact.
6. Protecting the Graft
Seal the Graft: Apply grafting sealant or wax to prevent moisture loss and disease.
Labeling: Label with the date and varieties of rootstock and scion.
7. Aftercare
Watering: Keep well-watered but avoid waterlogging.
Sunlight: Place in indirect sunlight to avoid stressing the graft.
Pruning: Remove shoots below the graft union to direct energy to the scion.
8. Monitoring and Maintenance
Check the Graft: Inspect regularly for new growth and signs of failure.
Remove Tape: Once healed, remove the tape to prevent girdling.
Support: Provide support if needed to protect the graft.

{Reactions}
💯

[9/15/2024 11:49 PM] asanford1984
Tips for Success
Practice Makes Perfect: Grafting improves with practice. Experiment with different techniques.
Healthy Materials: Use healthy, disease-free rootstock and scion.
Patience: Allow time for the graft to heal and grow. Don't be discouraged by initial failures.
By following these steps, you can successfully graft fruit trees, enjoying the benefits of improved fruit quality and tree performance.

[9/18/2024 7:50 PM] ratyr2
I would tend to disagree with Asanford_1984 on where anti GMO comes from.  Certainly there is a lot of hate from Monsanto practices, but I think most hate is not because of them.  Most of the hate I've experiences is from people believing that "playing God" is what is wrong with GMOs.  People eat food, and therefore are easily scared into believing that genetically modified food is going to be bad for them.  Now I don't know where a lot of the anti GMO attitude started from, but my first guess is the organic foods crowd.  The push for organic food was to remove anything artificial from our food, and when GM foods came on the scene they quickly added GM foods to the list of artificial.  I suspect that is where the start of the anti GMO came from.  You may want to consider looking into the organic foods history and see when they started saying no to GMOs and see how that connects.  Lots of Hollywood types tend to be on the organic/green end of the spectrum, so they might be the starts and large source of the pop-culture push against GMOs.

[9/21/2024 3:27 PM] asanford1984
Yes, it is true that apples originated in Kazakhstan. The wild ancestor of modern apples, 'Malus sieversii', is native to the forests of the Tien Shan mountains in Kazakhstan. These wild apples exhibit a wide variety of flavors, including some that taste like roses, strawberries, popcorn, and anise, among other unique flavors. This genetic diversity in wild apples provided the basis for the development of modern cultivated apples.

As for modern apples, it is also true that the majority of commercially grown apples can be traced back to a few ancestral trees. The genetic bottleneck in apple cultivation means that about 90% of the apples we eat today can be linked to just a couple of key ancestral trees. This limited genetic base is a result of selective breeding for desirable traits like taste, storage capability, and resistance to disease.

Regards,
Dr. Adarsha Gowda,
Chairperson of Entrepreneurship & Consultancy 
Head, Dept of Food Science,
Dept of Food Processing,
St Aloysius (Deemed to be University)
Mangaluru, Karnataka, India.

Gene guns are incredibly useful tools in the field of genetic engineering and allow you to get DNA into cells very easily. Electroporation
Electroporation, or electropermeabilization, is a technique in which an electrical field is applied to cells in order to increase the permeability of the cell membrane. This may allow chemicals, drugs, electrode arrays or DNA to be introduced into the cell (also called electrotransfer).
In microbiology, the process of electroporation is often us...

[9/26/2024 3:16 AM] toooonyyy
👉 👈  what if we used Edge Computing to make every farm Smart?


[9/26/2024 3:17 AM] toooonyyy
if you're curious about this, the basics are:


[9/26/2024 3:18 AM] toooonyyy
* use sensing devices to monitor and create data from agricultural sites: cameras, CO2 detectors, humidity
* pre-process that data somehow (either on the sensor device itself or once data is collected)
* use meaningful data to make predictions or assessments about the state of the farm, or otherwise inform your decisions about how you will farm in the future


[9/26/2024 3:18 AM] toooonyyy
an example i can think of


[9/26/2024 3:18 AM] toooonyyy
I'm not a plant scientist or anything but


[9/26/2024 3:19 AM] toooonyyy
Almond Trees sometimes produce nuts off season (i think I heard someone colloquially call them Mummy Nuts)


[9/26/2024 3:19 AM] toooonyyy
These nuts can create disease and other troublesome things for farms


[9/26/2024 3:20 AM] toooonyyy
You can use cameras, computer vision and machine learning to identify the prolific-ness of these off-season nuts.


[9/26/2024 3:21 AM] toooonyyy
This is not an easy task for machine learning either, as images of bare almond trees with little tiny nuts hanging off of them are really quite difficult to identify— especially for humans, but we really want *superhuman* performance out of artificial intelligence systems.


[9/26/2024 3:21 AM] toooonyyy
if you are curious about more, just reverse image search this and i'm sure you can find the exact project I'm talking about :)

[10/1/2024 7:23 PM] asanford1984
Understanding the vital role of soil testing in addressing plant nutrition deficiencies is crucial for farmers and agronomists. By delving into the pivotal role of soil testing in remedying plant nutrition deficiencies and optimizing crop yield, we can appreciate its significance in ensuring sustainable agricultural practices:

 Accurate Identification of Nutrient Deficiencies

Inaccurate visual symptoms of nutrient deficiencies, such as yellowing leaves or stunted growth, can sometimes lead to misinterpretation or confusion with other issues like pest damage or water stress. Soil testing aids in precisely identifying the nutrient levels in the soil, facilitating accurate diagnosis and targeted interventions. For instance, distinguishing between nitrogen and sulfur deficiencies, which often manifest similar symptoms, becomes possible through soil tests.


[10/1/2024 7:23 PM] asanford1984
Precision in Fertilizer Applications

Without proper soil testing, the application of fertilizers can be a hit-or-miss, resulting in the over-application of some nutrients and the under-application of others. Soil tests reveal nutrient imbalances, ensuring that fertilizers are applied at the appropriate rates. This not only corrects deficiencies but also prevents nutrient toxicity while reducing input costs.

Mitigating Long-Term Soil Depletion

Continuous cropping without a thorough understanding of the soil's nutrient status can lead to long-term depletion of vital nutrients. Regular soil testing provides insights into nutrient trends over time, enabling farmers to take corrective measures before deficiencies become severe. Sustaining soil fertility over multiple growing seasons leads to long-term productivity.

Customized Nutrient Management for Different Crops

Different crops have distinct nutrient requirements, and soil testing aids in tailoring nutrient management practices accordingly. By comprehending the nutrient needs of specific crops, farmers can ensure that their crops receive the necessary nutrients at crucial growth stages. For example, soil testing can uncover the need for additional phosphorus for root development in crops like maize or soybeans.

 Best Practices for Soil Testing

Regular soil tests, conducted at least once a year or between growing seasons, allow for tracking changes in soil fertility. Combining soil testing with plant tissue testing provides a comprehensive understanding of nutrient availability and plant uptake. Based on soil and plant tissue test results, the use of fertilizers and soil amendments can be tailored to specifically address nutrient deficiencies and imbalances.

[10/4/2024 4:44 PM] asanford1984
You can make snake plants, aka mother-in-law's tounge, bloom by adding these mixtures into the plants, and i suspect that itll work on other plants too.
Rice method
1.You should take two of rice fists and put them in a liter of water.
2.The grains should rest for two hours while we move on to another step.
3.Mix two tablespoons of coffee with half a liter of water and stir.
4.After these two steps, strain the rice and combine the two liquids.
5.Finally, we will use that water to water the mother-in-law’s tongue.
Pasta method 
1.Bring the pasta to a boil and cook it, you should not salt it because otherwise your liquid will burn the plants
2.Strain the paste and separate the liquid that resulted
3.Let macerate in a bowl
4.After the liquid is at a natural temperature you can use.
5.Apply in a complementary way in your plants after applying fertilizers to maximize their potency
https://homegardening.maiseconomia.online/2022/08/11/apply-this-liquid-to-your-mother-in-laws-tongue-to-make-it-grow-and-bloom-beautifully/?fbclid=IwAR25pgqDF4xf3ReViqLgG7GQ2fOIuC3RqA-rMyKjrM9LvH-H4y7rFj33a2Y

[10/5/2024 7:37 AM] asanford1984
Did you know that, 

The Ancient  Greeks discovered that the seeds of the 
carob (Carob, Ceratonia siligua) always have the same weight (about 0.20 g) and that's why they used it to measure the weight of precious metals .. 

The negotiations were with the Arabs who heard from the Greeks the word (wooden horns) carats (carats) The well-known carats of gold .. 

The word karat first entered the English language in the mid-15th Century. It derived from the French word carat which in turn came from the Italian carato. 
The word almost certainly derives from the Arabic qīrāṭ which came from the Greek kerátion meaning Carob seed. 
In Greek and Roman times it was used as a unit of weight …

If you want random changes, this might work but it's not much different from what already happens in the wild. 
Gamma rays are everywhere and pass through everything, and break DNA causing random mutations anyway. 
Most of the time, the DNA just gets repaired as is so there is no mutation.
It CAN work but you will probably just get mutations that cause the plants to die or be unhealthy. (all-most of the time)

[10/16/2024 11:25 PM] guyforgetting
Be careful with the UV.  I think you said it would be in a sealed environment, which is good, but it can still create ozone gas which can irritate your respiratory system (from experience).  So just make sure you have good ventilation after running the UV-C.  Sounds like an interesting experiment tho, you'll have to dial in the exposure time to achieve just the right amount of mutation.  Maybe try it with something cheap and fast growing like microgreens.

[11/2/2024 11:01 AM] lengie_
'Culture media should contain at least 25-60 mM of inorganic nitrogen for adequate plant cell
growth. Plant cells may grow on nitrates alone, but considerably better results are obtained
when the medium contains both a nitrate and ammonium nitrogen source. Certain species
require ammonium or another source of reduced nitrogen for cell growth to occur. ' from: https://phytotechlab.com/media/documents/TechnicalLiterature/ComponentsOfTissueCultureMedia.pdf

Murashige and Skoog medium (or MSO or  MS0 (MS-zero)) is the most popular plant growth medium used in the laboratories worldwide for cultivation of plant cell culture.

[12/1/2024 6:08 AM] rpd5203
i agree with mega and barrel, PEG is used more for protoplast fusion rather than getting protoplasts. cellulase and pectinase remove the cell walls to get protoplasts.
you could use physically methods like a blender or homogenizer and osmotic shock, you are more likely to damage protoplasts and its less efficient that the enzymes.

[1/2/2025 5:26 AM] ebo1744
Absolutely.  There is also a difference between precision and accuracy.  All tools have a design purpose, and other off-label purposes which they can work well at (or poorly at).  For the card sorter, I would have looked at adding a vacuum cup to the end of a spring-loaded syringe - so that a silicone cup can gently suck against the card to pick it up, and the spring-loaded syringe will allow the robot to be off in the z axis by quite a lot.  From there, you can use the same technique that pick-n-place robots use to spin and reorient parts before placing them on a stack for imaging.


[1/2/2025 5:31 AM] dowhilegeek
Yep, did suction jobby with bellows and it was all sprung and everything. I had more trouble with xy precision. Eventually itd start rubbing card edges on the pickup and destination trays after it went to a specific spot to look at the card from both sides, so the cards didnt have to be oriented right, and then there was a little flipper that would orient the cards in the done stack all the same way


[1/2/2025 5:32 AM] dowhilegeek
It wasnt a six axis arm, so it couldnt flip the cards over without an assist from a separate system


[1/2/2025 5:33 AM] dowhilegeek

[1/2/2025 5:33 AM] dowhilegeek

[1/2/2025 5:34 AM] dowhilegeek

[1/2/2025 5:48 AM] ebo1744
Yea, you can pick up a small CNC router for relatively cheap, and build the vacuum pickup as a loadable tool.  If you look around, sometimes you can pick up the oddest things for nothing, and then fix them.  I got my Techno-Isel CNC router that way -- blown controller, and I replaced it with an old PC and LinuxCNC.  I need to rebuild that again (15 years later), and I will tune the motor controller a bit better.  The original servos would do something like 1,300IPM (or 22" a second), and would hold a couple of thou position.  Anyway, I would have to think about what was actually necessary for movements, so you might be able to get away with only 4-axis (the 4'th being rotation of the tool/gipper).

[1/5/2025 7:32 AM] unheardhealer
Ozone is an oxidizing agent and may affect the different components of the media- I wouldn't be surprised if it broke down PGR.  I have thought about ozone sterilisation for bioreactor systems  before at work - either through gas or through ozonated water (basically bubble ozone through the water until it is saturated). I was also going to set up a small sterilsation box so that I could sterilise glassware/ etc instead of autoclaving- as I had seen someone try and make a commercial product for that purpose and I thought it would be good for sterilising some 3d printed prototypes that couldn


[1/5/2025 7:32 AM] unheardhealer
tb autoclaved


[1/5/2025 7:34 AM] unheardhealer
if you wanted to sterilise water for washing/ diluting bleach etc I think it would be a great idea-you could sterilise large volumes quite easily.

[1/6/2025 11:59 PM] raketula_37621
Nymphaea.


[1/7/2025 12:01 AM] raketula_37621
The purpose of pollen germination in artificial nectar is to test the pollen for viability. In this case, however, it is a series of tests to determine the optimal recipe for the artificial nectar.


[1/7/2025 12:03 AM] raketula_37621
Many Nymphaea hybrids have sterile pollen, so it is good to know whether a hybrid's pollen is viable or not before crossing.

[1/11/2025 6:13 AM] ebo1744
I have a collaboration with some wetland scientists who were bemoaning the time it takes to make soil rootbags.  Over the pandemic, I worked with one of them, and we used a sausage stuffer to make the root ingrowth cores, and we were able to get ~25x speedup in the number of cores we could make in an hour.  Then our collaborators in China modified a grout-pump and a rice-cake maker to do even better.  We are now sourcing a portable bulk transfer machine that I should be able to further streamflo the process...  In the past the below-biomass samples took a technician most of a year to make all the yearly samples.  Now, 2 people can crank out the same number in a few days.  It is a way of looking at everything around you not as it is, but what it is made out of and how you can modify it to do something you need.  It is hard to get some of these things published, but we do...

[1/11/2025 4:22 PM] raketula_37621
A little update on my experiments with water lily pollen: pollen treatment with 0.1% 2,3,5-triphenyltetrazolium chloride (TTC) to test pollen viability. TTC is metabolized in pollen to produce the red pigment 1,3,5-triphenylformazan. This means that any red colored pollen was viable (before testing).

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1327658855026524240/TTC_Pollen_Test.jpg?ex=6af336a9&is=6af1e529&hm=45579a4240b6f54011c5e157f18ccc5babc3a0cdb1340fab25eb9f3161dcbf4b&

{Reactions}
❤️ (5) 🔥 (2) 👀

[1/11/2025 4:24 PM] raketula_37621
The used pollen was stored for 18 month at -20°C before testing


[1/11/2025 4:39 PM] ebo1744
I'm assuming that the pollen is still viable after the treatment, or is this just done to make sure that you have *some* viable pollen in the lot?


[1/11/2025 4:47 PM] raketula_37621
Sadly not. The pollen will die due to the treatment. So far I use pollen germination tests to check for pollen viability of new water lily hybrides, but recent tests with different mixtures of artifical nectar showed, that a germination highly depends on the correct amount and ratios of incredients in the nectar. That means that I could have viable pollen, which doesn' t germinate if using the wrong nectar. With this TTC test (or a combination of TTC and germination), I get better test results of pollen viability. If the TTC test is positive and the germination test negative, I know, that I need to use a different nectar.

{Reactions}
👍

Not sure that might be done...  Hmmm... that reminds me that there is a video of one of the national databases that has a lab that verifies seed viability.  I thought there was some technique that they could use to check rare seeds without killing it.  That might be a place to look...

[1/11/2025 5:54 PM] raketula_37621
In my picture are some few pollen, which germinated even with TTC. Maybe if the concentration of TTC is small enough, some pollen survive, but the disadvantage of TTC is, that it needs time. I tested a 4 hour duration and a 24 hour duration with TTC and with more time, the red color becomes more intense. With 4 hours, it was difficult to distinguish between stained and unstained pollen. The picture above was from the 24 hour test.


[1/11/2025 5:56 PM] raketula_37621

{Reactions}
🍿 💀

[1/11/2025 7:30 PM] .megacyber
Why are you separating out the viable stuff anyways?


[1/11/2025 8:17 PM] raketula_37621
Example: If the maximum amount of pollen grains you can put on a stigma to pollinate a flower is 10,000 and you have pollen viability of 0.1%, then you only have 10 viable pollen. All dead pollen blocks the stigma, resulting in only 5 viable pollen being successful in the end. With such a low rate, there is a high risk that the plant will stop fruiting prematurely. Now we come up with the idea of ​​sorting viable and non-viable pollen and only using the viable ones for pollination. You take 1,000,000 pollen with the same viability as before. If you manage to find all 1000 viable pollen, sort them out and use only them for pollination, the chance of successful fruit setting and mature seeds is much higher

{Reactions}

[1/26/2025 2:23 AM] propag8er
So I asked chat gpt based on all available research to provide the best media composition for the elongation phase of chestnut. [2/13/2025 7:51 AM] ebo1744
Anyone here done any work with Physostegia virginiana, or any Physostegia?  Physostegia virginiana is native to my region, and I participate on a working group to help sort out germination and life history information for Physostegia correllii (which is endangered).  I am picking up some Physostegia virginiana seeds to try to work out a sterile TC seed protocol, and then will try it with P. correllii.


[2/13/2025 7:52 AM] ebo1744
No, but I have been looking for labs that can do full genome plant sequencing.  Let me know if you know of any place.


[2/13/2025 11:40 AM] palustral
We work with Physostegia virginiana, but just with seed, not tissue culture.  It is super aggressive.  We seed it on the top of trays in the fall, keep the trays outside under screens and it germinates in the spring.  We transpant those to 38 deep cell plug trays and plant those, or shift them up to 1 gallon containers.  Or, we add it to seed mixes.  You just need to make sure the seed stays on the soil surface.  If it is covered with soil, it does not germinate well or at all.  It also has a single cold dormancy, here.  It should be easy to grow in tissue culture.  I know there are several patented clones in the nursery trade.

[2/13/2025 5:20 PM] palustral
We added the screens to keep the rodents out.  I have used fridges for cold moist stratification.  We placed the seed in a mix of promix bx slightly moist and kept it in the fridge for 30 days.  This was then spread very thin across 3'x2' seed trays.  After germination and 2-3 true leaves we transplanted them into plug trays.  Great greenhouse work in the snow!  Seed source is critical for cold stratification we have found.  It is opposite of what you expect.  Seed from the north needs LESS stratification, where my seed from the lower midwest needs LONGER stratification.  We think that ecologically our region developed stronger or longer dormancies due to many freezes and thaws per winter.  A dormancy that is harder to break would germinate later in the spring and not grow during and early thaw.  The northern seed naturally freezes once and thaws once in the spring.  The further north you source your plants the weaker the statification required.  Not at all intuitive or expected until you think through the seasons in the various regions.  I live in the bootcamp for native plants in Missouri.

[3/16/2025 4:14 PM] ebo1744
Using laser cutters for cutting explants does not make sense to me.  To explain, when we use bleach to sterilize them, we cut away the chemically burnt parts before placing in the medium.  So why does using a laser make sense -- it will burn/damage the surrounding tissue.  I would love to be proven wrong, as lasers would make things a LOT easier than wielding scalpels.

💯

[3/31/2025 1:19 AM] rpd5203
if they are small/young and have something that no one else is doing, its advantageous to be a bit secretive until you have a little something to show for it and or have patents in hand. at around that time they should be screaming their heads off on what they are doing so the big corps notice them


[3/31/2025 3:38 PM] plantsinjars
It’s not that they’re being intentionally secretive—it’s just that the people working at those companies often struggle to explain their work in plain terms. Maybe it’s a side effect of too much time in school, or maybe it’s a desire to sound smart. But instead of just saying, “we’re developing a treatment for glaucoma,” they’ll say something like, “we’re characterizing the binding affinity of protein X to a regulatory enzyme involved in intraocular pressure modulation”—as if the average person has any idea what that means or why they should care. It’s just a pet peeve, and my experience is purely anecdotal, but it happens a lot.

{Reactions}

[3/31/2025 4:15 PM] palustral
Something about that makes me smile.  I left graduate school after being offered a Phd fellowship.  I grabbed my masters, a chainsaw, and a skidloader and went to work.  Universities teach a professional language, but the smartest (certainly not me) put this language into terms we all understand without even sounding like they are using a foreign language.  I go to many meetings and quitely listen.  Rather than naming a disease found in a landscape, I say root rot etc.  Tell the story not the specifics.  People that get caught in specifics, miss the mark for the message.  Often, they are smarter than the rest of us, but they are not relatable.  The smart relatable ones manage to gain a large following!  Hats off to your cool group.  I have learned a lot, here!

{Reactions}
👍 (4)

[5/6/2025 2:10 AM] am00019
This is of interest to me. I was reading the polploidy page on the AOS site. How do you know what level your orchids are?


[5/6/2025 11:01 AM] parasyte82
We can talk about environmental stuff in here?


[5/6/2025 2:01 PM] palustral
It is done by dying root tip cells and carefully counting the chromosomes in many cells.  There are several databases that track various species.  I do NOT currently have the equipment to make the counts, so I set seed pods and find my 3N plants quick because none of their seed germinates.  Those are gift or plants I sell.  3N plants are typically easier to grow and have produce great flowers.  I also buy plants that have been counted as tetraploids from other breeders.  Eventually I will buy a good microscope, but I do not need it now.

This project actually does minimal lab work, and yes, CLas is still unculturable. We begin injections at my research grove near Tampa in June. We harvest in December, then take the fruit over to UF CREC in lake Alfred for fruit weight, brix, fruit drop, canopy, and residue testing.


[5/13/2025 5:31 AM] amishbarbholland
Also almost all citrus in Florida is already being injected with OTC, after 180 days residues are well below 10 PPB, which is the federal requirement

[5/13/2025 9:55 PM] plantsinjars
@lopolis to answer your question, whether or not a plant keeps it's variegation in tc depends on whether the variegation is genetic or chimeric


[5/13/2025 9:55 PM] plantsinjars
an example of genetic variegation is a calathea. it will always have the same variegation pattern


[5/13/2025 9:56 PM] plantsinjars
Most expensive tropical aroids have chimeric variegation


[5/13/2025 9:56 PM] plantsinjars
There are many types of chimeric variegation, this is a good explanation


[5/13/2025 9:56 PM] plantsinjars
https://propg.ifas.ufl.edu/03-genetic-selection/04-genetic-chimera.html


[5/13/2025 9:57 PM] plantsinjars
Whether a chimeric plant maintains it's variegation in tc will depend which cells the baby plants are regenerating from


[5/13/2025 9:57 PM] plantsinjars
if they are regenerated from all all-white portion of a sectoral variegated plant, you end up with 100% variegated clones


[5/13/2025 9:57 PM] plantsinjars
different chimeras have different levels of stability


[5/13/2025 9:57 PM] plantsinjars
Philodendron Red Anderson is chimeric, but tends to be very stable in tc


[5/13/2025 9:58 PM] plantsinjars
other plants like the Monstera Albo have very unstable variegation, and usually lose it in tc or end up all green, which is why you don't see as many Monstera Albos grown in tc (compared to thai cons, which are widely tc'd)


[5/13/2025 9:59 PM] plantsinjars
I have a video outline written about the topic but it's too boring and dry rn, so I need to work some magic on it before I film it

[6/5/2025 8:15 PM] cleverguns
I have a question about fertilizers!


[6/5/2025 8:15 PM] cleverguns
I am using liquid ferts


[6/5/2025 8:18 PM] cleverguns
My tap water is about 8.3 ph and has a hardness of around 160ppm. For this reason I use RO water as a base. Coming from the aquarium world, RO water tends to be unstable and we typically remineralize it before adding it to the fish tank. The remineralizer has calcium chloride, potassium sulfate, magnesium sulfate anhydrous, and sodium bicarbonate. This way you can have water with the hardness you need (obv connects to ph and stability) and you know exactly what minerals and electrolytes are in your water (instead of tap).


[6/5/2025 8:19 PM] cleverguns

They are just so widespread that it’s difficult to control them. There’s not much we can do naturally about the bacteria in the tree.

Angel, ACP is an invasive pest that destroyed Florida’s multimillion (billion?) citrus industry. Yes, it has natural enemies and extensive research has been done of course. Both Florida and California Ag departments, as well as citrus grower organizations, are investing millions into this research.

[8/29/2025 1:58 AM] beer30247
I think the pressure from the air pump would pop a deli lid off or at the very least flex it enough to where the bottom cup couldn't displace the media enough to work.  It takes more force to hold the cup down than I expected.   If you wanted to make a cup to hold the explants and just feed them by tilting you could take a deli container and cut it down and poke holes in the bottom.  Put that cut one inside another one.  But what I'm doing is automated feeding using air pressure to raise the media level up to feed the explants.  Then release the pressure to lower the level.  The common way is to use 2 jars and force air in one or the other to move media between jars.  Explants in one jar, other jar holds the media when it's not feeding time.  But that takes up double the space.


[8/29/2025 2:05 AM] erwin_myco
No I mean getting two deli cups, hole in the bottoms and bold those together with the bottoms together, with PP attachments, then add something like a stainless straw between that part and the masonjar lid to press the whole thing down, add air, and use as a way to lift everything out.


[8/29/2025 2:09 AM] erwin_myco
The superthin deli cups might still be to weak to hold for a long time though


[8/29/2025 4:22 AM] beer30247
Possibly.  Would need more than one straw to hold everything down tight, high temp epoxy to bond everything.  Then a container to fit it all in.  I did learn that if the bottom cup isn't held down tight and level the air just escapes out one side and the water level doesn't go up enough. Wouldn't even need stainless just small aluminum tube would work as a hold down, it's not gonna come in contact with the media much if any.  The deli containers after you cut the bottom lip off would probably be too weak but do it in a large enough container and you could just put the whole thing in with some small holes drilled for the air.  Get too large of container and then your bottom "air cup" won't displace enough water without keeping the level right below the basket.  
The whole thing has been done Plantform.se sells everything but they aren't cheap and not everyone needs or wants something that large.  I'm just trying to make something more entry level.


[8/29/2025 4:24 AM] beer30247
I have thought about making the air cup and basket one piece.  But if you flip the basket over and use nodes that have a tiny leaf you can let the stem hang through the basket and keep the explants in place that way vs all bunched up.


[8/29/2025 5:06 AM] erwin_myco
Made a quick mockup with stuff I have on hand. The 2 delicontainers bolted together with the bulkhead thingy seem to be sturdy enough, and these are pretty flimsy deli containers. They don't fit the jar, but I pushed them under water without issue. Could find a straight straw or tube, but that can be fitted in the same way to the lid. An airfilter on the top of the lid above the straw and one beside it to bleed air. Some holes in the side of the top deli container to let liquid in, and blow through the straw for immersion. I think this should work as a cheap and easy TIB.

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1410822975858544711/CheapTIB.jpg?ex=6af33309&is=6af1e189&hm=34f14884941d86c176ef921f7ba6b8e20fd5bc033a01e568fc38f1a779216e3c&


[8/29/2025 5:10 AM] erwin_myco
Bit more of an investment, but using the double container type, but using 2 square containers similar to the plantform type with equal footprint and that stack well. Is probably a lot easier to work with in the long run, and more space efficient.


[8/29/2025 6:39 AM] beer30247
Ya that should work if the big container there is pp.  Need some holes in the side of the top deli container of course.  Big question is will that center tube hold the bottom deli down well enough. Then you get to adapt the straw down to an air pump of some kind. The fish tank pumps don't let air bleed back through so you need a valve to bleed off pressure.  Normally open solenoid works as it can go on the same timer as the pump.


[8/29/2025 6:41 AM] beer30247
The larger square containers would be more efficient for sure if one needed that many of one type of plant.  If I end up liking the tis system style of growing I'm sure I'll end up building bigger ones.


[8/29/2025 6:45 AM] beer30247
I went small as people seem to like the bio coupler thing and they are small.  Plus most can find mason jars locally.  If not I can always change up sizing to fit other jars if another size is popular in another country.


[8/29/2025 6:51 AM] beer30247
I thought about going the diy cut up containers route and after hunting the web for awhile I said F it I'll just design my own.  I back burner-ed this project back when the bio coupler things come out as i didn't have an enclosed printer, well now that I do I can finally make them.

[9/8/2025 8:18 PM] palustral
I have a UV flashlight that makes many contaminations on or in plants glow.  I bought the light to harden UV epoxy, but it is handy with the plants.  New chlorophyll glows magenta.  That is really handy with my seed flasking work. Many diseases glow various colors.  I have some plants with a crown rot that I will photograph in the dark with this light tonight.  Quick way to find problems and issolate or dispose of problem plants.

Monocots (bananas, grasses, gingers et cetera) are usually propagated by just dividing the roots, or by seed

ive been reading up on how grafted plants can exchange (sometimes significant amounts) of genetic material from the rootstock species to the scion... if you use a graft adapter you can get weird hybridization events between even different families. that made me wonder.... what if you took protoplasts from different species and just let them grow together for a while, maybe with enzymes added to media to slow down how fast they regenerated the wall, and then you induced shooting to separate out the two plants? how much genetic exchange could you reasonably expect to see?


[12/13/2025 7:29 PM] palustral
That is a very interesting line of thought.


[12/13/2025 7:35 PM] nyoksol
mostly siRNA, miRNA, tiRNA that move across, along with plasmids.... although, i wouldnt put full chromatid exchange out of the picture, especially if there was a bunch of dead cell goop floating around between the membranes. but in grafts thats enough to alter epigenetic factors. seems from what im reading that the cell walls would rebuild pretty quick, but if you had a well-mixed sample the plasmodesmata would maintain exchange between the cell groupings for quite a while


[12/13/2025 7:36 PM] nyoksol
probably need at least a few iterations shoot induction to make sure you had a pure culture and purge any chimeric lines


[12/14/2025 6:07 PM] dtflow
@ebo1744 @ebo1744 true. But this could be a valuable way of inducing targeted mutations with grafting. Making small changes in real time.

[12/21/2025 7:13 PM] ebo1744
There is a HUGE debate around all of this.  The real fear, and I think it is a valid one, is controlling things that escape into the wild.  I think just about every country in the world has plants that has escaped cultivation, or even GMOs that were in a test facility that somehow got out.  When citizens are doing this for fun, without any training, it gives pause to even the more daring of us.  There are also people who care about maintaining historic food crops and varieties, and you have the whole organic, non-GMO, folks, and it can become very difficult to maintain  heirloom and native landrace lines. I could probably go on for an hour or two about Monsanto suing organic farmers who they claimed they stole their patented seeds, only to discover a decade later that in fact their corn was want in fact sterile and easily crossed with the non-GMO corn.  I really hope that the families of the farmers that lost everything could get something back from Monsanto... Also, there is the whole thing about BT corn (which produces an enzyme that kills most soil organisms and pests, and they marketed it as safe, but eventually killed the soil)...  How do we, as citizen scientists, deal with unintended consequences?  I feel an ethical responsibility for what I do.

[12/21/2025 10:55 PM] vultus._.
This is not specifically what you’re looking for but here they find the specific way some aphids create galls https://www.janelia.org/news/heres-how-insects-coax-plants-into-making-galls

{Embed}
https://www.janelia.org/news/heres-how-insects-coax-plants-into-making-galls
Here’s How Insects Coax Plants into Making Galls
Scientists have identified proteins in aphid saliva that can alter plant development. These proteins drive abnormal growths called galls, which give insects a protected place to feed and reproduce.


[12/21/2025 10:56 PM] vultus._.
“When Stern and his team sequenced the genomes of aphids that made green galls and those that made red galls, they pinpointed a gene that varied between the two genomes. Aphids with one version of a gene that they named “determinant of gall color” made green galls; aphids with a different version made red ones. The finding piqued their curiosity, as the gene didn’t look like any previously identified genes.”

Webmaster-MGNV

Identifying Trees by Their Bark
Woody plants can be identified upon close examination of their bark. Variations in color and texture of bark, as well as other characteristics, provide helpful clues to a plant’s identity. Read on to learn more about bark.
https://images-ext-1.discordapp.net/external/RC6uxtzn-RFySRk7rPQZFItKnd-BpF7NdLP90Cd8464/%3Ffit%3D661%252C419%26ssl%3D1/https/i0.wp.com/mgnv.org/wp-content/uploads/2020/01/BEST-BARK-COLLAGE.png

{Reactions}
❤️

[12/30/2025 5:55 PM] shuhmaoue7929
Every tree's bark tells a story


[12/30/2025 9:13 PM] shuhmaoue7929
*The bark of each type of tree has evolved to adapt to the environment in which the species grows. For example, the thick bark of Sequoiadendron giganteum (giant redwood) contains no flammable pitch or resin, allowing the tree to survive intense fires. In contrast, some pine trees, such as Pinus contorta (lodgepole pine), produce flammable oils in their bark because the high temperature of fire releases seeds from their waxy cones, aiding reproduction. The volatile oils in Betula ssp. (birch) bark make it waterproof and resistant to decay.*


[12/30/2025 9:14 PM] shuhmaoue7929
*Other trees produce chemicals in their bark to make them unpalatable or to ward off competition from other tree species. The tannins of Quercus ssp. (oak) trees and acetogenins of Asimina triloba (pawpaw) are powerful natural insecticides. The oxalate crystals contained in the inner bark of conifers destroy the mouthparts of insects, and the bitter, almond-scented cyanogenic glycosides in the bark of Prunus serotina (black cherry) deter browsing animals such as beavers. The essential oils in bark shed by Betula nigra (river birch) inhibit the growth of neighboring trees.*


[12/30/2025 9:15 PM] shuhmaoue7929
*Bark texture also provides protections. Smooth bark repels climbing plants. The exfoliation of bark in birches, for example, allows them to shed the lichen and moss that form on their trunks. The young green branches of trees are thought to be photosynthetic, but Populus tremuloides (quaking aspen) is unique among North American trees in that it can photosynthesize through its greenish-gray bark even after its leaves drop.*

[1/6/2026 8:48 PM] zap233

[1/6/2026 9:16 PM] afieldofflowers
this uses agrobacterium, which inserts a piece of the DNA it carries within its genome into a plant cell, similar to how human retroviruses put pieces of their genome into that of human cells (agrobacteria is a plant pathogen that cause galling ((plant cancer)) in infected plants). 

im not sure if its a random insertion or if they prefer to target specific plant genome locations for insertion. either way this sort of modification is called transient modification because it is temporary (the inserted modification dies with the modified cell, this modification does not have the ability to propagate to other/new cells unless you transform germline cells with agrobacterium (see: arabidopsis floral dip))


[1/6/2026 9:17 PM] afieldofflowers
RUBY is a reporter gene, it tells you that your transformation protocol worked in a very quick and easy manner 
https://zhaolab.biosci.ucsd.edu/ruby/

{Embed}
Michael Mudgett
https://zhaolab.biosci.ucsd.edu/ruby/
RUBY

[1/6/2026 9:18 PM] afieldofflowers
no, the agro used in scientific research has been heavily attenuated to remove as much of the other genome parts that cause plant disease

Even if the meristem is transformed it won't continue?


[1/6/2026 9:19 PM] afieldofflowers
the only things left in the genome of the modified agro are the parts that are needed to move part of its genome (the part of the agro plasmid which you modify) into a plant cell

[1/6/2026 9:21 PM] afieldofflowers
the entire point is to maintain the integrity of the modified plasmid sequence so you can transform plants with it later


[1/6/2026 9:22 PM] afieldofflowers
and everything else which does not help with that is discarded


[1/6/2026 9:22 PM] afieldofflowers
thats what the antibiotics in this kit are for, to maintain the integrity of the plasmid which is carrying the RUBY reporter gene

[1/7/2026 3:42 PM] apteronotus5947
You do that though to quickly verify whether your process worked. As another example of you engineer a bacteria to make some cool protein you want to study you also insert, for example resistance to kanamycin antibiotics, so when you grow your cultures in media with kanamycin you know the procedure was successful before scaling up the culture. For an ornamental trait and plants you might be able to just visually confirm though.

[1/10/2026 7:27 PM] henrygrasman
I’ve got a batch of Synsepalum
dulcificum growing. It has the annoying tick of staying small for 1.5yr about before actually growing. Basically 3-4 leaves and then it stops.

I knocked a leaf off one on accident and noticed that it attempted to grow back 3 more, but they all stopped growing at various small stages. I am guessing this means the growth is inhibited by hormones produced in the leaves.

The roots seem to grow very slowly. I suspect there is a threshold of size that breaks the slow growth cycle. 

I have a plant to experiment with and either:
1. attempt to stimulate growth of additional foliage with the appropriate hormones 
2. Attempt to stop the leaves from producing whatever inhibits growth
3. Attempt to grow a larger system of roots which will presumably cause the plant to mature

I don’t want to mess with more than one of these, so does anyone have suggestions for what avenue I should pursue?


[1/10/2026 7:31 PM] zap233
Consider the possible reasons it's evolved a slow seedling stage in its natural habitat.  What are the resources or conditions that create a limiting factor?


[1/10/2026 7:42 PM] henrygrasman
The proposed mechanism that resonated with me was that the plant exists in a very aggressive dry-wet cycle. It grows minimal foliage to survive the dry, and grows roots as quickly as possible (very slowly) until the roots are of adequate size and depth to draw from the water table, at which point it produces foliage. 

Since the transition cannot be triggered by water (or the occasional  storm would bait the plant into over growing) it is triggered by time/root size. 

So… I need the roots to grow faster (making 3rd option appealing), but they may already be growing as fast as they can, limited by energy from the small foliage, not hormones. 
So perhaps growing the foliage hormonally will allow the roots to grow faster, and the plant will not die because my house does not experience dry spells. 

That is my thought process.


[1/10/2026 8:14 PM] zap233
Makes sense to me, I like how much thought you've put into this.  Good luck!

{Reactions}

[1/12/2026 4:55 AM] apteronotus5947
I promise not to spam too many weird photos but here are two better monstera node microscope photos. The green is from the outer skin tissue on the node, and my best guess is the needle-like structures in the cells are the calcium oxalate crystals, known as raphides. 

Deeper in the node is the other photo where you can see xylem vessels, tubes that transport water and nutrients for the plant. To support the plant it also produces lignin, which here you can see in the long chair of ovals known as annular pattern. This helps give the monstera strength and support to grow all over your house 💪.

Side problem, even on variegated albos, the albino patches can only get a few inches in size before they start to wither (the entire structure of the plant does not account for nutrients flowing INTO the leaves).

[1/30/2026 6:19 PM] henrygrasman
I’m not taking a side here but plants as air purifiers are not working with dust. They’re claimed to remove VOCs, which has been demonstrated multiple times in literature. A HEPA particulate filter is completely ineffective in this domain, unless it has additional elements like activated carbon etc. 

Ventilation (bringing air in from outside the house) is the effective method to address VOCs practically. A plug in air filter circulating air is no substitute for a properly set up hvac intake. And it is the *plants* outside that eventually clear them from the air. The plants indoors are doing this too, there just aren’t enough plants usually


[1/30/2026 6:19 PM] henrygrasman
So… go just outside and touch grass might be the answer after all

{Reactions}
👆 😂

You could hypothetically do this by fusing a haploid cell with a diploid cell in somatic hybridization, it which from there you’d eventually get to do it with all the chromosomes available, and each hybridization would follow with somehow in whatever way splitting all of the successful hybridizations, and then from there having pairs of mixed and matched diploid cells, along with hopefully haploid cells, you’d then just need to get the haploid cells to also fuse together once more so you are only left with diploid cells. You could simply keep “card shuffling” the chromosomes like this over and over until they stabilize


[5/1/2026 10:26 PM] vultus._.

Pendimethalin
Pendimethalin is a selective herbicide of the dinitroaniline class used preëmergently and postemergently to control annual grasses and certain broadleaf weeds. It inhibits cell division and cell elongation. Pendimethalin is approved in Europe, North America, South America, Africa, Asia and Oceania for different crops including cereals (wheat, b...
https://images-ext-1.discordapp.net/external/jmthe3ACDM8wR0i0o5TNoJakeaVIcdxwqectr1zI7Rw/https/upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Pendimethalin_v2.svg/1280px-Pendimethalin_v2.svg.png


[5/4/2026 10:53 PM] vultus._.
Oh well I think it would make sense with inhibitors because you don’t want to totally cease all function and kill the cells


[5/4/2026 10:54 PM] nyoksol
okay back up, the way polyploidization works is you bind the microtubulin. which stops the cells from moving their chromosomes around during anaphase, and so when they divide instead of each cell getting half by the end of mitosis, you end up with one cell getting everything (twice what it should have =4x), and the other not getting any (and promptly dying)


[5/4/2026 10:55 PM] nyoksol
any polyploidization agent will do this. colchicine is the one most often used, and it sucks because its very selective for animal microtubulin and not for plant. so you need high doses, its toxic to the handler, and it doesnt work very well, so you often get partial polyploids or chimeras or just nothing at all because the plant dies from the dose before it can actually disrupt mitosis


[5/4/2026 10:55 PM] nyoksol
the dinitroanilines are much more selective for plants which reverses all the issues, and they work a lot better


[5/4/2026 10:56 PM] nyoksol
there's some newer research using oryzalin, i couldnt find any available on the market so i subbed for trifluralin, but the mechanism of action is the same

[5/9/2026 1:03 PM] vultus._.
I wouldn’t say them being in the petioles has to be an issue, that’s only an issue in a specific group of plants, and with ethanol the viscosity of the liquid is lower than even water which makes it able to get down even deeper, and if you still see some bubbles or something you can just give it a light squeeze to get it down without harming the plant, and either way even if the first round of ethanol doesn’t do it the second round surely will


[5/9/2026 1:07 PM] vultus._.
If I have something that gets into the roots or likes to hide I tend to just dispose of the soil and just soak the entire plant in a mixture of ethanol and dish soap and that usually gets rid of anything (although that’s for small plants)


[5/9/2026 1:10 PM] vultus._.
If it’s root rot I’m just using hydrogen peroxide, or bleach on a severe case but in that case I’d just be putting localized bleach on the specific spots where I can see the worst infections

[5/9/2026 2:37 PM] vultus._.

[5/9/2026 2:38 PM] vultus._.

[5/13/2026 8:33 AM] lefteyedominant
I'm not an expert but I rotated with a chloroplast researcher. Apparently, chloroplast transformation in monocots is in a hurdle right now. From what I understand, it's because monocots don't need photosynthesis genes as much as dicots during callus stage. So the resistance markers that would help a gene to spread in dicot chloroplasts don't spread as well in monocot chloroplasts.

[5/28/2026 7:48 AM] nyoksol


{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1509433253613011075/Screenshot_20260510-132533_MuPDF_mini.jpg?ex=6af35903&is=6af20783&hm=49fe244b3e682eb5fe14dbff941f4de660bde619ef7d0dd8f89bb0c58d11b6df&


[5/28/2026 7:49 AM] nyoksol
These are the macros and micros for passionflowers, for instance


[5/28/2026 5:34 PM] zap233
That doesn't tell us what it is so we have no idea.


[5/28/2026 5:36 PM] zap233
MS is used so commonly because it is cheap and provides a very good balance of nutrients to most plants.


[5/28/2026 5:36 PM] zap233
It's purpose made for tissue cultures unlike a normal fertilizer, which is usually just supplementing soil.


[5/28/2026 5:37 PM] zap233
I would assume finding the correct balance would be difficult, and you'll likely see macro nutrients too high and be too low on micro nutrients


[5/28/2026 5:39 PM] zap233
If you have absolutely no way to get tissue culture nutrient mixes like MS, and want to go this way, just be aware it will take lots of trial and error.


[5/28/2026 5:40 PM] zap233
I would find a few that contain as many of the micro nutrients as you can, and try at least several different concentrations to find what they grow best in.


[5/28/2026 5:41 PM] zap233
If you are just starting out doing tissue culture, this will make things more complicated and difficult for you, that's why it's best to start off with something that is known to work.


[5/28/2026 6:02 PM] nyoksol
If you want a decent ad-hoc mix, look into banana mash and coconut milk based protocols


[5/28/2026 6:03 PM] nyoksol
But otherwise dont expect good results without MS, especially if youre just throwing things together without a systematic plan

[5/29/2026 9:23 PM] nyoksol
Having made my own polyploid basils you can definitely tell if it took


[5/29/2026 9:24 PM] nyoksol
Morphology is pretty visibly different. Way thicker leaves and stem, strong aroma (if it has one), and you can count stomatal density under a basic microscope if you want something quantitative. Polyploids end up having 1.4x stomata per square unit (sqrt 2 if youre wondering)


[5/29/2026 11:18 PM] vultus._.
I have a microscope but it’s like ridiculously sketchy


[5/29/2026 11:19 PM] nyoksol
you can see stomata at 400x, so just the 40 objective


[5/29/2026 11:20 PM] vultus._.

[5/29/2026 11:20 PM] nyoksol
im using one of those cheap 40 dollar microscopes i had someoen give to me lol


[5/29/2026 11:20 PM] nyoksol
i did growing tip applications


[5/29/2026 11:20 PM] vultus._.
I think datura specifically would very much benefit from being polyploid


[5/29/2026 11:20 PM] nyoksol
you basically want a low dose spread out over as long a time period as possible so you can hit the meristem cells while theyre dividing


[5/29/2026 11:21 PM] nyoksol
we're talking like, 5 ml in between the leaves when they're small


[5/29/2026 11:21 PM] nyoksol
i did it once a day for 3 consecutive days and had a maybe 10% suspected conversion (haven't validated yet). my dose was definitely too small because i had 0 fatalities too


[5/29/2026 11:21 PM] vultus._.

[5/29/2026 11:23 PM] nyoksol
unless your solution is REALLY dilute


[5/29/2026 11:23 PM] nyoksol
the 5 ml at 1 mM (yes) burns them pretty bad

oh sorry everything above i meant uL

200 uL kills everything

[6/16/2026 3:29 AM] littleike222
Random question for you guys that are much smarter than I am. I have liquid prop drops and powder rooting hormone (can provide ingredient list if needed). 
Would I be able to encourage root growth to TC plugs by adding the prop drops to my normal water or just use the rooting hormone powder on them?


[6/16/2026 3:25 PM] juzzy__
May depend on the plant, but in general by increasing the light (intensity or length) will satisfy the shoot growth and help naturally develop more roots. I'm not sure how much effect IBA has on plants that already have roots


[6/16/2026 3:26 PM] juzzy__
google says Adding IBA (indole-3-butyric acid) to water for plants that already have roots is generally not recommended and can be harmful. IBA is a specialized hormone used to trigger new root cell formation in cuttings. On mature roots, it can stall growth or stunt the plant.

[6/27/2026 9:18 PM] nyoksol
Wondering if anyone might know anything about this. Kava has been domesticated from somatic mutations vegetatively propagated for thousands of years. It is sterile, unlike its wild ancestor. The major cytological study I found says that the chromosome count is identical to the wild plant, although four of the chromosomes are significantly enlarged (probably enzymes related to kavalactone production). Meiosis is successful, no issues pairing or forming tetrads or separating, but pollen grains have a damaged appearance, so it seems they spontaneously abort


[6/27/2026 9:19 PM] nyoksol
Is there anything you could do about that? I'm getting mixed readings on whether that might indicate an issue with the nourishing tissue that sustains the haploid pollen grains or just non-viable cells post-meiosis

[7/3/2026 8:32 PM] vultus._.
All species in the datura lineage are highly poisonous and contain extremely potent doses of hundreds of active compounds, most commonly being referred to as the main danger being the plethora of tropane alkaloids


[7/3/2026 8:34 PM] vultus._.
Some highland strains of datura wrightii can actually survive around -25 degrees Celsius, but it’s important to note that the entire top portion of the plant is still killed, it simply regrows from the root stock


[7/3/2026 8:36 PM] vultus._.
Some datura species will also just wilt and die at 10 degrees Celsius


[7/3/2026 8:41 PM] vultus._.
Along with this, no part of the plants I grow will ever be let back into the local ecosystem and any plant matter I do end up with as a byproduct will be composted and reused for other datura substrates


[7/3/2026 8:41 PM] vultus._.
Datura are also legal to grow in most countries, including mine


[7/3/2026 8:41 PM] vultus._.
Okay enough legality and safety rants

[7/9/2026 7:56 PM] nyoksol
so, ive been going back and forth with opus 4.8 on modeling various conditions of somaclonal variation and mutagenesis, to see how much you can actually increase diversity through intentional selection.... you get some transient fitness loss, but recombination helps to drop the negative genes and escape before the genome gets too degraded, and you can get back up to wild-type fitness levels (or even higher under some conditions). the interesting thing im seeing is just how *sensitive* the genomes are to degradation under even moderate mutagenic conditions

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1524836681931296929/6974a31e-1fcf-4814-9b6f-7985d54a1400.png?ex=6af35ad3&is=6af20953&hm=e94084db4b38232c812624cd4dfa646e008371725cedb95bd2358065b0d026c1&


[7/9/2026 7:57 PM] nyoksol
at least with somaclonal variation, most of the changes you get are epigenetic, some revert after crossing, and some are more stable, but in either case you dont see much change in the underlying genetic sequence


[7/9/2026 7:58 PM] nyoksol
the changes you do see are from increased transposase activity caused by transient demethylation, and if you get lucky they'll insert into a regulatory region and give you !!fun!! and interesting structural changes


[7/9/2026 7:59 PM] nyoksol
i guess the goal is to get to a position where you get a bunch of mutations then do aggressive cross-breeding to purge out the negative variants; the fitness increase looks nice but also reflects environmental adaptation (ie domestication), so it would probably still be underneath the actual fitness under wild-type conditions, where selection is for selectability (if you hyper-adapt and then your environment changes, you die out 🙂 )

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1524837539750346752/1fc01e60-0fec-42d9-b0a0-6b395d962134.png?ex=6af35b9f&is=6af20a1f&hm=144dfa6be94f503373b1c839172c55bd130154563183e49ead687c3bdbeb777f&


[7/9/2026 8:03 PM] nyoksol
this simulation was really interesting. the differences aren't super dramatic between different mutagenic methods (point vs structural vs shock vs somacloncal) in terms of how much diversity you get, but the impact on genome quality is super dramatic, and fertility drops the fastest with anything involving double strand breaks, chromosomal inversions, etc because of meiotic incompatibilities that cause trouble with pairing. so radiation is probably off the table, at least with double strand break radiation (still might be able to get away with point-mutation inducing radiation)

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1524838300932636713/9df8de45-201e-49b7-bd66-c3a1718c8784.png?ex=6af35c55&is=6af20ad5&hm=87f81afaae8d5f773fc9b853bbbbca49a0811e0ce6eacfd735eaca547a664ba7&


[7/9/2026 8:05 PM] nyoksol
the actual space of fitness vs morphological diversity differs pretty dramatically too between the different methods. radiation gives very high variation but massively kills overall fitness. somaclonal variation and shock factors (mostly epigenetic) are pretty good at maintaining fitness and can give decent morphological change. point mutations seem to be the only way to really induce beneficial morphological changes, and even then its raaaare

{Attachments}
https://cdn.discordapp.com/attachments/1242118808337125468/1524838971736326395/05843631-15c5-4c61-be85-35957deb62a7.png?ex=6af35cf5&is=6af20b75&hm=3e87dccc7c904d1cdbb1409227226684f76516cc217f4e2d2d0555a5f24adef2&


[7/9/2026 8:06 PM] nyoksol
the parameterizations are all pretty sensitive to the number of explants you're able to actually work with, i modeled at 300 which might be on the low end, along with the selective pressure, because if you can purge more strongly you can push a higher mutation rate without incurring genomic degradation


[7/9/2026 8:08 PM] nyoksol
i strongly suspect minmaxing this would cause issues with disappearing gradients and strong non-linearities


[7/9/2026 8:09 PM] nyoksol
but i guess my takeaway is that you probably need a really big working population to be able to introduce any significant amount of variation without degrading the genome quality so much that the population ends up worse off long-term


[7/9/2026 11:16 PM] microplastics.enjoyer
So it seems like the important parts are the method you select for traits, the size of the gene pool, and the intensity of the mutagen? It seems like this is a method to functionally speed up domestication, which would be interesting for some edibles/proto crop plants. How well would this work for introducing fertility/robustness if you only have a single individual to get genes from?


[7/10/2026 2:08 AM] infernoblaze47
@nyoksol can I get the tldr


[7/10/2026 2:08 AM] infernoblaze47
lol


[7/10/2026 4:22 AM] nyoksol
basically im trying to see if i can reasonably model conditions where you start with a small founding population, and through various mutagenic techniques try to create a larger population that has a sustainable amount of genetic diversity


[7/10/2026 4:22 AM] nyoksol
the idea would be applying this to rare/endangered species to help 'restore' some of what we've destroyed by speeding up evolutionary timescales


[7/10/2026 4:32 AM] nyoksol
yeah so the surprising thing im seeing is that the optimizer actually strongly prefers *just* somaclonal variation. EMS mutagenesis and radiation (or anything analogous) just adds too much genetic load, too quickly for purification to keep up, so while you might get impressive results over the first two to three generations, eventually the load catches up and you get mutational meltdown


[7/10/2026 4:32 AM] infernoblaze47
oh thats cool


[7/10/2026 4:32 AM] nyoksol
the initial gene pool in the model is 4 distinct genotypes and while you get more initial drift if you start lower it doesnt change the fundamentals of the population dynamics, so surprisingly you can actually start with near-zero diversity and build a good amount


[7/10/2026 4:34 AM] nyoksol
fertility: this is tricky, because once you lose fertility you can't have recombination, and recombination is super important for purifying deleterious genes. if you have even low but non-zero fertility, you can ramp it up really quickly in just a few generations, the issue starts more when your starting point is literally zero


[7/10/2026 4:45 AM] microplastics.enjoyer
So what causes the mutations in tissue culture? Is it that different from plants just growing normally? And this would seem to say that anything kept long enough in tc is going to be mutated


[7/10/2026 4:52 AM] nyoksol
somaclonal variation, you just get random lucky mutations when cells divide, and cells divide faster in vitro than in a real plant


[7/10/2026 4:52 AM] nyoksol
especially in a callus because its dedifferentiated growth


[7/10/2026 4:53 AM] nyoksol
plus, dedifferentiation reduces methylation, and stress (eg cold, heat, oxidation, dehydration) also reduces methylation


[7/10/2026 4:53 AM] nyoksol
and reduced methylation turns on transposase genes that go and chop up the genome by inserting themselves into random places in the genome (jumping genes!) and possibly breaking things and possibly making things work better


[7/10/2026 4:53 AM] nyoksol
so its a combination of genetics and epigenetics


[7/10/2026 4:54 AM] nyoksol
if you keep a callus proliferating too long without ever doing shoot induction you do eventually lose regenerative potential


[7/10/2026 6:05 AM] nyoksol
updated model results came in, and are both really exciting and a little disappointing


[7/10/2026 6:06 AM] nyoksol
you can maintain a stable population with consistently increasing genetic diversity relative to a small founder stock (~4 plants but you can start as low as 1) in just 8 subculture generations, by keeping 500 plants in culture at any given time


[7/10/2026 6:07 AM] nyoksol
you can also get your founder stock up to nearly wild-type levels of genetic diversity within 14 subculture generations, but you need to maintain a pool of 4,000 to do that


[7/10/2026 6:08 AM] nyoksol
the model did converge pretty consistently here, you can't get large levels of genetic diversity without a large population to hold it, and thats under strong evolutionary conditions too. really makes you think about the ultimate fate of some species that have counts in the low thousands or even hundreds, if that.....

[7/13/2026 3:13 PM] doxatek
One example close to home to you that I like that really well illustrates the point is the eastern collared lizard. There are pockets of them native to Missouri. The habitat for them it's collapsing due to obvious reasons.

This lizard species is not wholly unique to Missouri. They're in Oklahoma, Texas, Arizona, and New Mexico etc. And the populations in some places are doing quite well. 

One idea was floated to bring some highly successful others in to with the native ones. They did. The resultant mixture and increase in genetic diversity happened. Aaand it nuked the population. Egg success dropped from 90 something percent to 60 percent. Behaviors changed as well which lost them the ability to survive in Missouri climate.

They had had their extremely closely tuned genetics and had it diluted and would have probably wiped them out

[7/23/2026 11:23 AM] noixdemascarade
Hey everyone!
I'm kinda new here, never did TC yet but I'd like to work in plant conservation (still doing my master's degree) and wondered if anyone had thoughts on using tc to preserve critically endangered species


[7/23/2026 12:26 PM] ekatsim01
I am currently working on that myself. The two biggest problems are: 
1. Humans are unreasonably afraid of TC .
2. Challenges with biodiversity


[7/23/2026 12:27 PM] noixdemascarade
biodiversity bc what you replicate is only one individual per explant I'm guessing?
Is creating and finding the right protocol a big challenge or not so much?


[7/23/2026 12:33 PM] ekatsim01
Well, every plant coming from a single explant is genetically identical, unless it mutates. But there are solutions. 

I have found very little information on culturing the plants native to my area.
I have been inventing my own protocols, based on similar species.  It is a matter of making an educated guess, and testing.

{Reactions}
❤️

[7/23/2026 12:34 PM] noixdemascarade
what solutions are you thinking of?
Taking samples from a sufficient number of plants for example?


[7/23/2026 12:40 PM] ekatsim01
Yes. A diverse sampling, preferably from the restoration location, because you can have different numbers of chromosomes in the same species, depending on the location. This is thought to be a result of adaptation. It is not well understood. 

Also, even a self pollenating plant, or two clones, will produce seeds with different DNA. 
The problem with this is when unfavorable traits are  inherited. 
So a combination is preferred.


[7/23/2026 12:46 PM] ekatsim01
For extremely rare plants, or areas that have none of the species you are trying to restore, you just have to do your best to work with what you have. 

If the plant is extinct and only one seed remains, you just grow and clone that. All while hoping it develops enough diversity in the future generations.


[7/23/2026 12:58 PM] ekatsim01
I think the chromosome thing is called polyploidy. 

 It is five in the morning. I usually wake up at 4AM, but I have not even gone to bed yet..
