[7/29/2026 8:54 AM] confusion_honeybee


{Forwarded Message}
Independent assortment is going to be rough with so many chromosomes, so the "sets" very likely wont separate together. You went from 2^8 gamete types (=256), to 3*8 gamete types (=6561)

Doesn't a complete knockdown of DMC1 cause infertility? Maybe I'm only thinking of animals, I'm not too sure about plants. 

Polyploidy also does weird things to the organisms, so the "traits" likely wouldnt be what you expect (ie, modern strawberries are octoploid from diploid/tetraploid ancestors, and look completely different). 

Another thing to look into - dosage issues (expanding on the last sentence). Where you had two copies of a gene before, now you have 6. This can cause a lot of issues when dosage matters (general issue with almost all polyploid individuals). You started with a F1 C1 individual, and ended with F F C1 C1 C2 C2 (or some variation thereof). the F set now has double the dosage, while the C set has anywhere from 0 to 4x, and anything shared between them could have as high as 6x. Adding to that it wouldn't be standard across the genome (Say, chromosome 1 from C1 is in the cross (F F C1 C1 C1 C1, 2x of F, 4x of C1, 0x of C2), but chromosome 2 differes (F F C1 C2 C2 C2. 2x of F, 1x of C1, 3x of C2). IIRC, polyploidy is much more successful when there arent dosage differences (although, plants do this much better than animals). 

"i just hope I can find the unique centromeric satellite repeats" 
This would probably be an interesting first step. Are the species sequenced with a reference genome assembled? How good is the reference genome (do they have high confidence that it's assembled properly?) Speaking from experience, repetitive regions and centromeric regions are a pain in the butt to assemble, as neither make sequencing/assembly easy. You also said that your cultivar may be a different cross than others, so youd also want to make sure that its your cross that was sequenced.
Originally sent: 7/28/2026 8:40 PM

[7/29/2026 8:09 PM] muddledmindedmagus
From my understanding, they're trying to resurrect two ancient cultivars of allium using a modern-day sterile hybrid between the two. Technically, the haploid genomes still mostly exist in the hybrid, but separating them out into the parental cultivars is a doozy. This is verging on "technically, it might be possible", but there are so many other things going on that reduce the chances. But, with the technology nowadays, it might actually someone feasible (if you are willing to spend a lot of time, and even more money on it). 

As someone who recently finished their PhD in genomics with a focus on a species complex where individuals sometimes hybridize (not plants, though), this is tickling all sorts of parts of my brain. It would be like trying to resurrect the two species that formed the Amazon Molly (although a bit more feasible, since its quite a bit more "recent")

fun fact, potatoes are an ancient (few MYA) hybrid between a plant in the tomato clade and a plant in the potato clade (cant find an open access but heres the original: https://www.cell.com/cell/abstract/S0092-8674(25)01019-0 )

now the REALLY interesting thing is that... neither species involved formed rhizomes. it was entirely transgressive segregation

[8/11/2026 9:53 PM] nyoksol
Dragon fruit will grow from cuttings whether you want it to or not. Just snip, let it callus over on a table for a day or two, and then throw onto a pot and remember to neglect it for a few months

Dragon fruit specifically will just branch and droop over everything that moves. People usually force it to climb up a trellis of some sort to develop a "stalk" and then let it trail off into a "canopy"

[9/17/2026 1:24 PM] ebo1744
If you look deeply into the Darling 58/54 study, you will find that there was a mixup in the lab's labeling of some of the trees, and last I heard they were not sure which ones carried the gene or not, and the this invalidated the scientific studies.  Assuming they got that straightened out (which could be done by scanning for  the gene in every tree and culling those that did not have it).  Wile I am not pedantic about non-GMO's, anyone who owns an organic farm will never be able to plant them on their land.  BTW, one of the big push backs from the organic folks is that when they pulled the gene, they took it from wheat, which makes it transgenic, and not another chestnut. They could have done surveys to find similar genes from both American and Chinese chestnuts, and there still would have been a bunch of push back but nothing like there is with transgenics. I am not sure what the organic community, and the governing's body, think about genetic surgery overall, maybe they would allow for a same species or same genera genetic surgery to impart the gene.  Probably not,  but it is worth an ask. I do however understand their concern about some of the wacky things people have done with transgenics. I have also seen some really cool things, like using plants to make medicines, but dod not put in an off-switch to make sure it cannot get into the wild...


[9/17/2026 1:29 PM] ebo1744
There is actually ongoing researc into finding endophytes that are known to be resistant to the blight, or the root-rust that also affects american chestnuts, and inoculate seeds and see how they do.  This is non-GMO work, but along the lines of "find a natural symbyot to help fight the disease"

[10/6/2026 4:02 PM] whit62_52058
https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2026/press-release/

{Embed}
https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/medicine/2026/press-release/
Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine 2026
The Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine 2026 was awarded jointly to Karl Deisseroth, Peter Hegemann and Georg Nagel “for their discoveries concerning light-gated ion channels and optogenetics.”
https://images-ext-1.discordapp.net/external/RF2rvEnVr43W_DCntpnwnd7mo9QVrhsxmsHXbjd_qvo/https/www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2026/10/NP_POM_2026_3-2_01-1024x683.jpg


[10/6/2026 4:02 PM] whit62_52058
“It all began with Peter Hegemann’s curiosity. He wondered how Chlamydomonas, a single-celled alga, is able to swim towards a light source. In the early 2000s, he and Georg Nagel discovered channelrhodopsin, an algal protein with unique properties, found on the surface of the cell. When it is illuminated by blue light, a channel opens through the protein. Charged ions then flow into the cell, creating an electrical impulse. They found that regardless of which cell they put the protein in, those cells became light sensitive.”


[10/6/2026 4:03 PM] whit62_52058
Used an algal protein to essentially create on/off switches for neurons to study the brain. Amazing stuff.
